Transport and Temperature 1: Exact spectrum and resistivity for the one-dimensional infinite- U U Hubbard model
Shuo Liu, Yuhao Ma, Hitesh J. Changlani, Philip W. Phillips, B. Andrei Bernevig

Abstract
Understanding charge transport in strongly correlated systems remains a central challenge in condensed matter physics, particularly in light of the ubiquitous linear-in- T T resistivity observed in strange metals across many platforms from bulk cuprates to twisted bilayer graphene. Here, we investigate charge transport in the one-dimensional Hubbard model in the infinite-interaction limit. Focusing on the dilute limit with a fixed number of doped holes, we first construct the exact and explicit - i.e. beyond Bethe ansatz energy spectrum and then derive a closed-form analytical expression for the charge Drude weight at arbitrary temperatures. We further analyze the low-temperature scaling and identify a linear-in- T T correction to the Drude weight. Upon regularizing the singular Drude contribution to the DC conductivity, we find that this behavior corresponds to an effective linear-in- T T resistivity, which may provide analytical insight into the emergence of strange-metal transport in two-dimensional strongly correlated systems.



Introduction.—
Since the experimental discovery of high-temperature superconductivity (SC) in cuprates several decades ago, a consensus on the underlying microscopic mechanism has remained elusive
Dagotto 1994
;
Damascelli et al. 2003
;
Phillips 2010
;
Davis and Lee 2013
;
Fradkin et al. 2015
;
Keimer et al. 2015
;
Kane et al. 1989
. One point of agreement is that the answer lies in the solution to the doped Mott insulator problem
Phillips 2010
. Central to this problem is the dynamical generation of a charge gap in half-filled band
Phillips 2010
;
Meinders et al. 1993
. In this regard, the two-dimensional (2D) Hubbard model
Anderson 1987
;
Zhang and Rice 1988
;
Qin et al. 2022
is viewed as the gold standard for the copper-oxide layer. Other popular models include the
t
-
J
model which approximates the dynamical physics of the Hubbard model. However, these strongly correlated 2D systems remain analytically intractable. Numerical simulations also face severe limitations, such as the limited system widths accessible to density matrix renormalization group
Stoudenmire and White 2012
or the sign problem in quantum Monte Carlo simulation
Loh et al. 1990
;
Troyer and Wiese 2005
;
Wu and Zhang 2005
;
Li et al. 2015
;
Li et al. 2016
;
Wei et al. 2016
;
Li and Yao 2019
;
Wan et al. 2022
;
Kim et al. 2020
. We also note recent developments using neural quantum states
Roth et al. 2025
;
Chen et al. 2025
;
Gu et al. 2025
.

An extreme limit of the
t
-
J
model is the
J=0
case in which the high-energy sector is completely decoupled. As
J
is inversely related to the coupling constant in the Hubbard model,
J=0
can be viewed as the infinite-interaction limit
Nagaoka 1966
;
Liu et al. 2012
. Even a single hole in this background is a non-trivial problem and the resultant transport properties remain unknown. For certain classes of lattices (there are now generalizations to honeycomb and diamond lattices
Bobrow et al. 2018
), the Nagaoka theorem tells us that a fully polarized ferromagnetic state is the ground state
Nagaoka 1966
.
Counterexamples to the Nagaoka theorem have also been investigated on frustrated lattices, including the
120^{\circ}
antiferromagnetic ground state on the triangular lattice
Haerter and Shastry 2005
;
Sherif et al. 2025
;
Sharma et al. 2025
and the exact resonating-valence-bond spin-liquid ground state on the checkerboard lattice
Glittum et al. 2025
.
Besides these analytical results, a number of intriguing phenomena have been reported in this infinite-interaction limit, based on numerical studies and experiments. These include kinetically induced hole–magnon bound states
Zhang et al. 2018
;
Morera et al. 2024
;
Navarro et al. 2024
;
Qiao et al. 2025
on the triangular lattice and, remarkably, signatures of strange-metal transport on the square lattice with a single doped hole
Fratini et al. 2025
. However, even in this infinite-interaction limit, more general situations such as multiple doped holes remain highly nontrivial and analytically challenging.

A natural starting point for analytically understanding the physics of the 2D Hubbard model
Anderson 1987
;
Zhang and Rice 1988
;
Qin et al. 2022
;
Xiao et al. 2023
;
Perepelitsky et al. 2016
;
Arovas et al. 2022
;
Gull et al. 2013
;
Werner and Millis 2007
;
Gull et al. 2010
;
Valentí and Gros 1992
;
Schüler et al. 2019
is its one-dimensional (1D) counterpart
Essler et al. 2005
. The 1D Hubbard model is integrable and thus admits an exact solution via the Bethe ansatz
Lieb and Wu 1968
;
Shastry 1986
;
Takahashi 1972
;
Takahashi 1974
;
Göhmann and Murakami 1998
;
Izergin et al. 1998
. Despite this integrability, the Bethe ansatz solutions are not explicit, and a comprehensive analytical understanding of charge transport remains highly nontrivial
Zotos 2005
;
Heidrich-Meisner et al. 2007
;
Bertini et al. 2021
. At half filling, the system is a Mott insulator, and consequently the charge Drude weight vanishes
Carmelo et al. 2018
;
Carmelo et al. 2013
;
Prelovšek et al. 2004
;
Karrasch et al. 2014
. Away from half filling, the charge Drude weight becomes finite
Shastry and Sutherland 1990
;
Stafford et al. 1991
;
Ilievski and De Nardis 2017
;
Ilievski et al. 2018
;
Fava et al. 2020
;
Zotos et al. 1997
;
Garst and Rosch 2001
.
Its exact expression at zero temperature has been obtained in Refs.
Shastry and Sutherland 1990
;
Stafford et al. 1991
;
Peres et al. 2000
. The leading finite-temperature correction was later derived from the finite-size corrections to the thermodynamic Bethe ansatz equations, yielding a
T^{2}
dependence
Fujimoto and Kawakami 1998
. However, the full temperature dependence of the Drude weight remains unknown, and no simple analytical expression has been established. Consequently, much of the existing understanding relies on numerical approaches
Stephan and Horsch 1990
;
Fye et al. 1991
;
Jin et al. 2015
;
Jeckelmann et al. 2000
;
Aichhorn et al. 2003
;
Kirchner et al. 1999
.

In this work, we focus on charge transport in the 1D Hubbard model in the infinite-interaction limit. We first construct the exact but more importantly
explicit
eigenstates and energy spectrum of the Hamiltonian. We then derive an analytical expression for the charge Drude weight at arbitrary temperatures with a single doped hole (see Sec.
III.1
), whose form becomes particularly simple in the large-system-size limit (see Eq. (
14
)). The result can be straightforwardly generalized to an arbitrary but finite number of doped holes (see Sec.
III.2
and Sec.
III.3
). In this dilute limit, where the number of holes
n_{h}
is kept fixed as the large-system-size limit is taken, the charge carriers propagate essentially independently. Consequently,

n_{h}
enters as an overall prefactor in the Drude weight.

More importantly, based on the analytical expression for the Drude weight with a single doped hole, we further analyze its low-temperature scaling behavior. We find that the leading temperature dependence of the Drude weight is linear in
T
, which translates into a linear-in-
T
behavior of the effective resistivity extracted from a broadened Drude peak. This observation, when generalized to 2D, may provide insight into the analytical understanding of the linear-in-
T
resistivity behavior associated with strange-metal phenomenology, as reported in numerical studies of the 2D Hubbard model
Deng et al. 2013
;
Pakhira and McKenzie 2015
;
Kokalj 2017
;
Vučičević et al. 2019
;
Huang et al. 2019
;
Fratini et al. 2025
and in experiments on cuprate superconductors
Phillips et al. 2022
;
Marel et al. 2003
;
Fournier et al. 1998
;
Dagan et al. 2004
;
Jin et al. 2011
;
Martin et al. 1990
;
Cooper et al. 2009
;
Daou et al. 2009
;
Collignon et al. 2017
;
Doiron-Leyraud et al. 2017
;
Legros et al. 2019
;
Brown et al. 2019
. We also note that SYK-type models can produce linear-in-
T
resistivity in the all-to-all interaction configuration
Chowdhury et al. 2022
;
Patel et al. 2023
;
Cha et al. 2020
;
Li et al. 2024
, and similar behavior has also been observed in twisted bilayer graphene
Polshyn et al. 2019
;
Wu et al. 2019
;
Cao et al. 2020
;
Jaoui et al. 2022
. Furthermore, we also derive an analytical expression for the Drude weight at finite doping
\delta
in both low- and high-temperature limits (see Sec.
V
), where
\delta=n_{h}/N
is fixed in the thermodynamic limit. In this regime, the Drude weight
exhibits a
T^{2}
correction, consistent with the result in Ref.
Fujimoto and Kawakami 1998
.
The change in the
T
dependence of resistivity from linear to quadratic as density of holes becomes finite shows the care needed to extrapolate any numerical results, especially in 2D where the system sizes attained are small, and hence, the need for analytical solutions.

Model and definition of optical conductivity.—
In this work, we consider the 1D Hubbard model in the infinite-interaction limit.
The Hamiltonian is given by

\displaystyle\hat{H}=-t\sum_{\sigma=\{\uparrow,\downarrow\}}\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\left(\tilde{c}^{\dagger}_{j+1,\sigma}\tilde{c}_{j,\sigma}+\tilde{c}^{\dagger}_{j,\sigma}\tilde{c}_{j+1,\sigma}\right),

(1)

where

\tilde{c}^{\dagger}_{j,\sigma}=(1-n_{j,\bar{\sigma}})\,c^{\dagger}_{j,\sigma}

denotes the projected creation operator for a fermion with spin
\sigma
at site
j
and

n_{j,\bar{\sigma}}=c^{\dagger}_{j,\bar{\sigma}}c_{j,\bar{\sigma}}

is the density operator for fermions with the opposite spin
\bar{\sigma}
, thereby enforcing the no-double-occupancy constraint. The hopping amplitude
t
sets the overall energy scale, and we set
t=1
throughout this work. We impose periodic boundary conditions (PBCs), such that

\tilde{c}^{\dagger}_{N,\sigma}\equiv\tilde{c}^{\dagger}_{0,\sigma},

where
N
is the total number of lattice sites. Moreover, we denote by
n_{h}
the number of doped holes in the system.

The real part of the optical conductivity (see Sec.
I.3
) can be expressed as

\displaystyle\mathrm{Re}\,\sigma(\omega,T)=2\pi D(T)\,\delta(\omega)+\sigma_{\mathrm{reg}}(\omega,T),

(2)

where
T
is the temperature,
\omega
the frequency,
\delta(\cdot)
the Dirac delta function,
D(T)
the temperature-dependent charge Drude weight corresponding to the singular contribution at zero frequency, and
\sigma_{\mathrm{reg}}(\omega,T)
the regular (finite-frequency) contribution. Here, “regular” indicates that this part does not contain a
\delta(\omega)
singularity at zero frequency.
The DC conductivity at zero frequency is given by

\displaystyle\sigma_{\mathrm{DC}}(T)=2\pi D(T)\delta(\omega)+\lim_{\omega\to 0}\sigma_{\mathrm{reg}}(\omega,T),

(3)

consisting of both the Drude weight contribution and the contribution obtained by extrapolating the regular part to
\omega=0
.

D(T)
and
\sigma_{\mathrm{reg}}(\omega,T)

(see Refs.
Shastry and Sutherland 1990
;
Maldague 1977
;
Baeriswyl et al. 1987
;
Scalapino et al. 1992
;
Kirchner et al. 1999
;
Abouelkomsan et al. 2025
or Sec.
I
for a detailed derivation) are given by

\displaystyle D(T)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\left[\frac{1}{2}\,\langle-\hat{K}\rangle-\frac{1}{Z}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m,n\\
E_{m}\neq E_{n}\end{subarray}}e^{-\beta E_{n}}\frac{|J_{mn}|^{2}}{E_{m}-E_{n}}\right],

(4)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\mathrm{reg}}(\omega,T)

\displaystyle=\pi\frac{1-e^{-\beta\omega}}{\omega ZN}\sum_{n,m}e^{-\beta E_{n}}\left|J_{mn}\right|^{2}\delta(E_{n}+\omega-E_{m}),

(5)

respectively. In Eq. (
4
) the first term is the diamagnetic response whereas the latter term is the paramagnetic contribution, both of which enter the DC response.
Note that the expression for regular part in Eq. (
5
) does not apply strictly at zero frequency, as is clear from the derivation presented in Sec.
I.3
. Here
\beta=1/T
is the inverse temperature,
\hat{K}
denotes the kinetic-energy operator, which coincides with the Hamiltonian
\hat{H}
in the present model (see Sec.
I
), and
\langle-\hat{K}\rangle=\mathrm{Tr}(-\hat{K}e^{-\beta\hat{H}})/Z

denotes its thermal expectation value, where

Z=\mathrm{Tr}\!\left(e^{-\beta\hat{H}}\right)
is the partition function,
E_{n}
is the eigenenergy of the
n
-th eigenstate
|n\rangle
of the Hamiltonian
\hat{H}
.
The charge current operator
\hat{J}
(see Sec.
II
) is

\displaystyle\hat{J}=i\sum_{\sigma=\{\uparrow,\downarrow\}}\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\left(\tilde{c}^{\dagger}_{j+1,\sigma}\tilde{c}_{j,\sigma}-\tilde{c}^{\dagger}_{j,\sigma}\tilde{c}_{j+1,\sigma}\right),

(6)

and its matrix elements in the eigenbasis of
\hat{H}
are defined as

J_{mn}=\langle m|\hat{J}|n\rangle.

Exact solutions of the energy spectrum.—

We have obtained the complete set of eigenstates and the energy spectrum explicitly. The detailed derivations including the explicit expressions for the eigenstates for the cases with one hole, two holes, and an arbitrary number of holes are presented in Sec.
III.1
, Sec.
III.2
, and Sec.
III.3
, respectively. Below, we present only the energy spectrum with a single doped hole.

In the fully polarized sector with

S_{z}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j}(n_{j,\uparrow}-n_{j,\downarrow})=\pm\frac{N-1}{2}
, corresponding to
N-1
spin-up fermions (or
N-1
spin-down fermions),
the energy spectrum (see Sec.
III.1.1
) is

\displaystyle E(m)=2\cos\!\left(\frac{2\pi m}{N}\right),

(7)

where
m=0,\dots,N-1
labels the distinct eigenstates.

More generally, in the sector with

S_{z}=\frac{N-1}{2}-n_{\downarrow}
and
0<n_{\downarrow}<N-1
,
where
n_{\downarrow}
denotes the number of spin-down fermions,
the energy levels take the form (see Sec.
III.1.4
)

\displaystyle E(m,m_{\downarrow})=2\cos\!\left(\frac{2\pi m}{N}+\frac{2\pi m_{\downarrow}}{N(N-1)}\right),

(8)

with
m=0,\dots,N-1
and
m_{\downarrow}=0,\dots,N-2
. The degeneracy of each such energy level is
\frac{1}{N-1}\binom{N-1}{n_{\downarrow}}
.
For finite-size systems, additional eigenvalues not captured by Eq. (
8
) may appear. However, as discussed in Sec.
III.1
, the number of such states is negligible in the thermodynamic limit and therefore does not affect the analytical results for charge transport presented below.

Table 1:
Analytical expressions for the charge Drude weight in different limits.

Case

High-temperature limit

Low-temperature limit

Single hole

D_{1h}(T\to\infty)=\dfrac{1}{NT}

D_{1h}(T\to 0)=\dfrac{1}{N}\!\left(1-\dfrac{T}{4}\right)

Fixed number of holes
n_{h}

D_{n_{h}}(T\to\infty)=\dfrac{n_{h}}{NT}

D_{n_{h}}(T\to 0)=\dfrac{n_{h}}{N}\!\left(1-\dfrac{T}{4}\right)

Fixed hole density
\delta=n_{h}/N

D_{\delta}(T\to\infty)=\dfrac{\delta(1-\delta)}{T}

D_{\delta}(T\to 0)=\dfrac{\sin(\pi\delta)}{\pi}-\dfrac{\pi T^{2}}{24\,\sin(\pi\delta)}

Analytical expression for the Drude weight.—
In this section, we present the analytical expression for the Drude weight building upon the explicit energy spectrum and eigenstates. We focus on the case of a single doped hole, and also discuss the cases of an arbitrary fixed number of holes and of fixed density of holes.

First, we note that in the present model the current operator
\hat{J}
commutes with the Hamiltonian
\hat{H}
, i.e.,
[\hat{J},\hat{H}]=0
(see Sec.
II.2
). This property is special to the infinite-interaction limit and does not hold for the 1D Hubbard model with finite interaction strength, where generally
[\hat{J},\hat{H}]\neq 0
. As a consequence,
\hat{H}
and
\hat{J}
share a common set of eigenstates
|n\rangle
, and thus we have

\displaystyle\ \ |J_{mn}|=0\ \text{when}\ m\neq n,

(9)

which is also true for degenerate states (see Sec.
II.3
).
Together with
\hat{K}=\hat{H}
for the present model,
the Drude weight in Eq. (
4
) simplifies to

\displaystyle D(T)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2N}\,\langle-\hat{H}\rangle,

(10)

and the regular part in Eq. (
5
) becomes

\displaystyle\sigma_{\mathrm{reg}}(\omega,T)

\displaystyle=\pi\frac{1-e^{-\beta\omega}}{\omega ZN}\sum_{n}e^{-\beta E_{n}}\left|J_{nn}\right|^{2}\delta(\omega).

(11)

Therefore,
\sigma_{\mathrm{reg}}(\omega,T)
vanishes at any nonzero frequency and thus
\lim_{\omega\to 0}\sigma_{\mathrm{reg}}(\omega,T)=0
. Consequently, according to Eq. (
3
), only the Drude weight shown in Eq. (
10
) contributes to the DC conductivity for the present model.

Moreover, although Eq. (
11
) is not directly applicable at strictly zero frequency, by setting
\omega=0
, we have

\displaystyle\sigma_{\mathrm{diff}}(T)

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi\beta}{ZN}\sum_{n}e^{-\beta E_{n}}\left|J_{nn}\right|^{2}\delta(\omega),

(12)

where we have used
\lim_{\omega\to 0}\frac{1-e^{-\beta\omega}}{\omega}=\beta
.
Note that
\sigma_{\mathrm{diff}}(T)\neq\lim_{\omega\to 0}\sigma_{\mathrm{reg}}(\omega,T)
.
It is related to the Drude weight through

\displaystyle\sigma_{\mathrm{diff}}(\omega,T)=2\pi D(T)\,\delta(\omega)-2\pi D_{m}(T)\,\delta(\omega),

(13)

that is, it measures the difference between the singular Drude weight and the Meissner stiffness
D_{m}(T)

Shastry 2006
;
Mukerjee and Shastry 2008
. In Sec.
IV.1.3
, we have proven that
\sigma_{\mathrm{diff}}(\omega,T)=2\pi D(T)\,\delta(\omega)
,
i.e.,
D_{m}(T)=0
, consistent with the absence of SC in one dimension.

In the sector with a single doped hole, the analytical expression for the Drude weight in the large-system-size limit reads (see Sec.
IV.1.2
)

\displaystyle D_{1h}(T)=\frac{\beta}{N}\left(1-\frac{I_{2}(2\beta)}{I_{0}(2\beta)}\right),

(14)

where
I_{l}(\cdot)
denotes the
l
-th modified Bessel function of the first kind, arising from the
\cos(\cdot)
energy spectrum shown in Eqs. (
7
) and (
8
). For a fixed number of doped holes
n_{h}
, in the thermodynamic limit, the Drude weight generalizes straightforwardly by acquiring an overall multiplicative factor of
n_{h}
(see Sec.
III.2
and Sec.
III.3
),
reflecting that, in the dilute limit, the holes propagate essentially independently. Beyond this limit, this does not apply. The analytical expressions obtained for the specific heat in Sec.
IV.1.4
and Sec.
IV.2.4
also support this point.
In the thermodynamic limit, the hole density
n_{h}/N
vanishes, and consequently the Drude weight also vanishes, consistent with the Mott-insulating nature of the system at half filling
Carmelo et al. 2018
;
Carmelo et al. 2013
;
Prelovšek et al. 2004
;
Karrasch et al. 2014
.

At finite doping with fixed
\delta
, although we do not obtain an analytical expression for the Drude weight
D_{\delta}(T)
at arbitrary temperatures, we derive analytical expressions for the Drude weight in both the low- and high-temperature limits (see Sec.
V
). The analytical expressions for the Drude weight for a fixed number of holes
n_{h}
and for a fixed hole density
\delta
in the high- and low-temperature limits are summarized in Table
1
.

As shown in Table
1
, at zero temperature,
D_{1h}(T=0)=\frac{1}{N}
and
D_{\delta}(T=0)=\frac{\sin(\pi\delta)}{\pi}
, both of which are consistent with the Luttinger-liquid prediction
\frac{Kv_{s}}{\pi}
, where
K=1/2
is the Luttinger parameter for the present model
Schulz 1990
;
Moreno et al. 2011
, and
v_{s}=\left.\frac{d\varepsilon(k)}{dk}\right|_{k=k_{F}}=2\sin(\pi\delta)
is the sound velocity. Note that with fixed
n_{h}
, the Fermi momentum scales as
k_{F}\sim n_{h}/N
, and the corresponding Fermi energy behaves as
E_{F}\sim k_{F}^{2}\sim n_{h}^{2}/N^{2}
. Therefore, the Luttinger-liquid description, which is valid as a low-energy effective theory only for
T\ll E_{F}
, applies strictly only at zero temperature.

More interestingly, at low temperatures as shown in Table
1
, the single-hole Drude weight
D_{1h}(T)
exhibits a linear-in-
T
dependence, whereas
D_{\delta}(T)
exhibits a quadratic-in-
T
dependence
Fujimoto and Kawakami 1998
. It gives pause to the extrapolations of conclusions from finite-size numerics. Especially in 2D where the density is fixed, the thermodynamic limit is far from being accessible.

Figure 1:
Normalized Drude weight
D(T)/D(0)
as a function of temperature
T
for (a) fixed density of holes
\delta=n_{h}/N=1/4
and (b) a single doped hole. In panel (b), the numerical Drude weight rapidly converges to the analytical prediction as the system size increases.
In panel (a), a clearly distinct scaling behavior is observed.
By fitting the data to the form
a+bT^{c}
, we find that the extracted exponent
c
is larger than
1
and approaches
2
as the system size increases.

We have numerically verified these two distinct scaling behaviors using ED. To compare different system sizes, we normalize the Drude weight by its zero-temperature value, i.e.,
D(T)/D(0)
. The results are shown in Fig.
1
. In Fig.
1
(b), corresponding to a single doped hole, the Drude weight obtained from ED rapidly approaches the analytical prediction (Eq. (
14
)) as the system size increases. In Fig.
1
(a), where the density of holes is fixed at
\delta=n_{h}/N=1/4
, a direct observation of the quadratic correction is limited by the exponential growth of the Hilbert space.
Instead, we fit the numerical data using the form
a+bT^{c}
and find that the extracted exponent
c
approaches the predicted value of
2
as the system size increases.
Therefore, the two ways of taking the limit do not commute.
As explained in Sec.
IV.3
, with a fixed number of holes the system is in the nondegenerate regime, whereas with a fixed hole density the system enters a degenerate Fermi-gas regime.

Linear-in-
T
effective resistivity.—

Next, we analyze the temperature dependence of the
DC resistivity
\rho(T)
which is given by

\displaystyle\rho(T)\delta(\omega)=\frac{1}{2\pi D(T)}.

(15)

Due to the singularity associated with
\delta(\omega)
, we first introduce the following regularization,

\displaystyle\delta(\omega)=\frac{1}{\pi}\lim_{\eta\to 0}\frac{\eta}{\eta^{2}+\omega^{2}},

(16)

i.e., replacing the Dirac delta function by its Lorentzian representation where
\eta
is a broadening parameter (damping rate). Then we discuss the temperature dependence of the regularized DC resistivity with fixed
\eta
,

\displaystyle\rho(T)=\frac{\eta}{2D(T)},

(17)

i.e., the effective resistivity extracted from a broadened Drude peak. Note that in numerical calculations of the DC conductivity in Eq. (
3
), which include both the singular Drude-weight contribution and the regular part, for finite-size systems of a generic model, due to the discrete energy spectrum, the Dirac delta function in Eq. (
3
) is also approximated by its Lorentzian representation, with the broadening parameter
\eta
chosen to be of the order of the mean energy level spacing
Mukerjee et al. 2006
;
Patel and Changlani 2022
in order to obtain a smooth conductivity. The thermodynamic limit is then inferred via finite-size extrapolation.

In the following, we focus on the case of a single doped hole; the analysis can be generalized to the cases of
n_{h}
doped holes or finite hole density
\delta
based on Eq. (
17
).
The temperature dependence of the effective resistivity defined in Eq. (
17
), together with the corresponding Drude weight, for temperatures
T\in[0,10]
is shown in Fig.
2
. Here we set the hopping strength
t=1
; therefore
T
should be understood as
T/t
.

We find two regimes exhibiting linear-in-
T
behavior. In the high-temperature limit,
according to Table.
1
,
one obtains

\displaystyle\rho(T)\approx\frac{N\eta}{2}\,T,

(18)

demonstrating a linear-in-
T
effective resistivity. Since we have proved that
2\pi D(T)\delta(\omega)=\sigma_{\mathrm{diff}}(T)
, based on Eq. (
12
), in the high-temperature limit the expectation value of
\hat{J}^{2}
becomes temperature independent, and thus the overall factor of
\beta
gives rise to the linear-in-
T
behavior.

More importantly, in the low-temperature limit,
according to Table.
1
,
the effective resistivity behaves as

\displaystyle\rho(T)\approx\frac{N\eta}{2}\left(1+\frac{T}{4}\right),

(19)

which again contains a linear-in-
T
dependence (see Fig.
2
(b)).

Strange-metal behavior remains a long-standing and central problem in strongly correlated systems
Phillips et al. 2022
. In particular, a recent numerical study
Fratini et al. 2025
reported a linear-in-
T
resistivity persisting down to temperatures as low as
T=0.02t
in the 2D Hubbard model with infinite interaction on an 18-site rotated square lattice with a single doped hole. Upon using the same extrapolation procedures as in
Fratini et al. 2025
, we have reproduced their finding

Liu et al. 2026a
.
The authors argued that the microscopic processes relevant to strange metallicity may already emerge at the single-hole level. Our 1D analytical example shows that extra care must be taken to identify all the components of conductivity (Drude vs regular) and infinitesimal behavior of resistivity (linear in
T
for 1D) vs finite filling behavior (quadratic in
T
for 1D). As 2D infinite limit is hard to achieve numerically, extension of analytic solutions proved here for the 1D case to 2D may provide useful insight into mechanisms that could underlie strange-metal behavior in higher-dimensional strongly correlated systems.

Figure 2:
Resistivity
\rho(T)
as a function of temperature
T
, with the overall factor
N\eta/2
removed. Left panel:
T\in[0,10]
. In the high-temperature regime, the slope approaches
1
.
Right panel:
T\in[0,0.2]
. In the low-temperature regime, the slope approaches
1/4
.

Discussion and outlook.—
In conclusion, we have obtained the explicit energy spectrum and eigenstates (see Sec.
III.3
) of the one-dimensional Hubbard model in the infinite-interaction limit and derived a closed-form analytical expression for the charge Drude weight in the dilute regime at arbitrary temperatures, as well as analytical expressions for the Drude weight at finite doping in both the low- and high-temperature limits. The resulting linear-in-
T
correction to the Drude weight at infinitesimal hole concentration translates into a linear-in-
T
effective resistivity extracted from a broadened Drude peak, which is relevant to the strange-metal behavior.
However, at finite hole concentration where the independent hole approximation begins to break down, the effective resistivity becomes quadratic due to the
T^{2}
correction to the Drude weight at low temperatures,
underlining the need for an analytic solution in 2D where finite filling extrapolations are difficult.

An interesting direction for future work is to develop perturbative frameworks built upon the one-dimensional exact solutions, with the goal of gaining analytical insight into the strange-metal behavior reported in the two-dimensional Hubbard model
Deng et al. 2013
;
Pakhira and McKenzie 2015
;
Kokalj 2017
;
Vučičević et al. 2019
;
Huang et al. 2019
;
Fratini et al. 2025
and observed in cuprate experiments
Phillips et al. 2022
;
Marel et al. 2003
;
Fournier et al. 1998
;
Dagan et al. 2004
;
Jin et al. 2011
;
Martin et al. 1990
;
Cooper et al. 2009
;
Daou et al. 2009
;
Collignon et al. 2017
;
Doiron-Leyraud et al. 2017
;
Legros et al. 2019
;
Brown et al. 2019
. Another promising direction is to seek exact solutions directly in the 2D Hubbard model
Liu et al. 2026b
or in the recently developed momentum-mixing variant of the Hatsugai–Kohmoto model
Mai et al. 2026
;
Ma et al. 2025
, for which combined numerical and analytical approaches remain feasible, and then to extract analytical insight from such solutions.

Supplemental Material for “Transport and Temperature 1: Exact spectrum and resistivity for the
one-dimensional infinite-
U
Hubbard model”

I
Linear-response derivation of the optical conductivity

In this section, we first review linear response theory in Sec.
I.1
, then use it to derive the optical conductivity in Sec.
I.2
. We next present the expressions for the Drude weight and the regular part of the optical conductivity in Sec.
I.3
, and finally give their Lehmann representations in Sec.
I.4
. For further details, see Refs.
Shastry and Sutherland 1990
;
Maldague 1977
;
Baeriswyl et al. 1987
;
Scalapino et al. 1992
;
Kirchner et al. 1999
.

I.1
Review of linear response theory

We briefly review linear response theory
Kubo 1957
.

We first consider a system in thermal equilibrium described by a time-independent Hamiltonian

\hat{H}
.
The corresponding equilibrium density matrix is

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}=\frac{1}{Z}e^{-\beta\hat{H}},

(S1)

where
\beta=1/T
,
T
is the temperature and
Z=\text{Tr}\ \!\left(e^{-\beta\hat{H}}\right)
is the partition function.
The thermal expectation value of an operator
\hat{O}
is defined as

\displaystyle\langle\hat{O}\rangle=\text{Tr}\ \!\left(\hat{\rho}\hat{O}\right).

(S2)

We now turn on a time-dependent perturbation
\hat{V}(t)
, so that the full Hamiltonian becomes

\displaystyle\hat{H}(t)=\hat{H}+\hat{V}(t).

(S3)

As a result, the density matrix becomes time dependent, which we denote by
\hat{\rho}(t)
.
Its time evolution is given by

\displaystyle\frac{d\hat{\rho}(t)}{dt}=\frac{1}{i\hbar}\left[\hat{H}(t),\hat{\rho}(t)\right]=\frac{1}{i\hbar}\left(\hat{H}(t)\hat{\rho}(t)-\hat{\rho}(t)\hat{H}(t)\right).

(S4)

The expectation value of operator
\hat{O}
also becomes time dependent

\displaystyle\langle\hat{O}(t)\rangle=\text{Tr}\ \!\left(\hat{\rho}(t)\hat{O}\right).

(S5)

To treat the perturbation more conveniently, we introduce the interaction picture.
For an operator
\hat{O}
, its interaction-picture form is defined as

\displaystyle\hat{O}_{I}(t)=e^{i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\,\hat{O}\,e^{-i\hat{H}t/\hbar}=\hat{S}^{-1}(t)\,\hat{O}\,\hat{S}(t),

(S6)

where
\hat{S}(t)=e^{-i\hat{H}t/\hbar}
. Similarly, the interaction-picture density matrix is defined as

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{I}(t)=e^{i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\,\hat{\rho}(t)\,e^{-i\hat{H}t/\hbar}=\hat{S}^{-1}(t)\,\hat{\rho}(t)\,\hat{S}(t),

(S7)

and the interaction-picture perturbation is

\displaystyle\hat{V}_{I}(t)=e^{i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\,\hat{V}(t)\,e^{-i\hat{H}t/\hbar}=\hat{S}^{-1}(t)\,\hat{V}(t)\,\hat{S}(t).

(S8)

We now derive the equation of motion for
\hat{\rho}_{I}(t)
.
Differentiating

\hat{\rho}_{I}(t)=\hat{S}^{-1}(t)\hat{\rho}(t)\hat{S}(t)
,
we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{d\hat{\rho}_{I}(t)}{dt}=\frac{d\hat{S}^{-1}(t)}{dt}\hat{\rho}(t)\hat{S}(t)+\hat{S}^{-1}(t)\frac{d\hat{\rho}(t)}{dt}\hat{S}(t)+\hat{S}^{-1}(t)\hat{\rho}(t)\frac{d\hat{S}(t)}{dt}.

(S9)

Using

\displaystyle\frac{d\hat{S}(t)}{dt}=-\frac{i}{\hbar}\hat{H}\hat{S}(t),\qquad\frac{d\hat{S}^{-1}(t)}{dt}=\frac{i}{\hbar}\hat{S}^{-1}(t)\hat{H},

(S10)

together with Eq. (
S4
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{d\hat{\rho}_{I}(t)}{dt}

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{\hbar}\hat{S}^{-1}(t)\hat{H}\hat{\rho}(t)\hat{S}(t)+\hat{S}^{-1}(t)\frac{1}{i\hbar}\left[\hat{H}+\hat{V}(t),\hat{\rho}(t)\right]\hat{S}(t)-\frac{i}{\hbar}\hat{S}^{-1}(t)\hat{\rho}(t)\hat{H}\hat{S}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{i\hbar}\hat{S}^{-1}(t)\left[\hat{V}(t),\hat{\rho}(t)\right]\hat{S}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{i\hbar}\left[\hat{V}_{I}(t),\hat{\rho}_{I}(t)\right].

(S11)

Integrating the above equation from an initial time
t_{0}
to
t
, we obtain

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{I}(t)=\hat{\rho}_{I}(t_{0})+\frac{1}{i\hbar}\int_{t_{0}}^{t}dt^{\prime}\,\left[\hat{V}_{I}(t^{\prime}),\hat{\rho}_{I}(t^{\prime})\right].

(S12)

This is an exact integral equation.
To make further progress, we iteratively substitute
\hat{\rho}_{I}(t^{\prime})
on the right-hand side.
To first order in the iteration,

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{I}(t^{\prime})=\hat{\rho}_{I}(t_{0})+\frac{1}{i\hbar}\int_{t_{0}}^{t^{\prime}}dt^{\prime\prime}\,\left[\hat{V}_{I}(t^{\prime\prime}),\hat{\rho}_{I}(t^{\prime\prime})\right].

(S13)

Substituting this back into Eq. (
S12
), we have

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{I}(t)=\hat{\rho}_{I}(t_{0})+\frac{1}{i\hbar}\int_{t_{0}}^{t}dt^{\prime}\,\left[\hat{V}_{I}(t^{\prime}),\hat{\rho}_{I}(t_{0})\right]+\left(\frac{1}{i\hbar}\right)^{2}\int_{t_{0}}^{t}dt^{\prime}\int_{t_{0}}^{t^{\prime}}dt^{\prime\prime}\,\left[\hat{V}_{I}(t^{\prime}),\left[\hat{V}_{I}(t^{\prime\prime}),\hat{\rho}_{I}(t^{\prime\prime})\right]\right].

(S14)

When the perturbation is weak, we keep only the term linear in
\hat{V}_{I}
, namely

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{I}(t)\simeq\hat{\rho}_{I}(t_{0})+\frac{1}{i\hbar}\int_{t_{0}}^{t}dt^{\prime}\,\left[\hat{V}_{I}(t^{\prime}),\hat{\rho}_{I}(t_{0})\right].

(S15)

We now assume that the perturbation is adiabatically switched on from the remote past,
so that
t_{0}\to-\infty
, and the system is in equilibrium at
t=-\infty
.
Since the equilibrium density matrix commutes with
\hat{H}
,
it is time independent in the interaction picture, and hence

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{I}(-\infty)=\hat{\rho}.

(S16)

Therefore Eq. (
S15
) becomes

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{I}(t)\simeq\hat{\rho}+\frac{1}{i\hbar}\int_{-\infty}^{t}dt^{\prime}\,\left[\hat{V}_{I}(t^{\prime}),\hat{\rho}\right].

(S17)

We now compute the time-dependent expectation value of operator
\hat{O}
. Using the cyclic property of the trace, the expectation value of operator
\hat{O}
can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\langle\hat{O}(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=\text{Tr}\ \!\left(\hat{\rho}(t)\hat{O}\right)

\displaystyle=\text{Tr}\ \!\left(\hat{S}^{-1}(t)\hat{\rho}(t)\hat{S}(t)\,\hat{S}^{-1}(t)\hat{O}\hat{S}(t)\right)

\displaystyle=\text{Tr}\ \!\left(\hat{\rho}_{I}(t)\hat{O}_{I}(t)\right).

(S18)

Using Eq. (
S17
),
we obtain

\displaystyle\langle\hat{O}(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=\text{Tr}\ \!\left(\hat{\rho}_{I}(t)\hat{O}_{I}(t)\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\text{Tr}\ \!\left(\hat{\rho}\,\hat{O}_{I}(t)\right)+\frac{1}{i\hbar}\int_{-\infty}^{t}dt^{\prime}\,\text{Tr}\ \!\left(\left[\hat{V}_{I}(t^{\prime}),\hat{\rho}\right]\hat{O}_{I}(t)\right)

\displaystyle=\text{Tr}\ \!\left(\hat{\rho}\,\hat{O}_{I}(t)\right)+\frac{1}{i\hbar}\int_{-\infty}^{t}dt^{\prime}\,\text{Tr}\ \!\left(\hat{\rho}\left[\hat{O}_{I}(t),\hat{V}_{I}(t^{\prime})\right]\right),

(S19)

where in the last equality we have used the cyclic property of the trace.

Since
\hat{O}_{I}(t)
is just the Heisenberg-picture operator evolved by the unperturbed Hamiltonian
\hat{H}
,
we may write it as
\hat{O}_{H}(t)
, and similarly
\hat{V}_{I}(t^{\prime})=\hat{V}_{H}(t^{\prime})
.
Moreover, for the equilibrium term,

\displaystyle\text{Tr}\ \!\left(\hat{\rho}\,\hat{O}_{H}(t)\right)=\text{Tr}\ \!\left(\hat{\rho}\,\hat{O}\right)=\langle\hat{O}\rangle,

(S20)

because the equilibrium expectation value is time independent.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\langle\hat{O}(t)\rangle=\langle\hat{O}\rangle+\frac{1}{i\hbar}\int_{-\infty}^{t}dt^{\prime}\,\left\langle\left[\hat{O}_{H}(t),\hat{V}_{H}(t^{\prime})\right]\right\rangle.

(S21)

It is convenient to define the deviation from equilibrium by

\displaystyle\delta\langle\hat{O}(t)\rangle\equiv\langle\hat{O}(t)\rangle-\langle\hat{O}\rangle.

(S22)

Then Eq. (
S21
) becomes

\displaystyle\delta\langle\hat{O}(t)\rangle=\frac{1}{i\hbar}\int_{-\infty}^{t}dt^{\prime}\,\left\langle\left[\hat{O}_{H}(t),\hat{V}_{H}(t^{\prime})\right]\right\rangle,

(S23)

which describes how the expectation value of an operator deviates from its thermal-equilibrium value under a time-dependent perturbation within linear response theory.

We now specialize to the case in which the time-dependent perturbation can be expressed as

\displaystyle\hat{V}(t)=\hat{A}\,f(t),

(S24)

where
\hat{A}
is a time-independent operator and
f(t)
is a time-dependent scalar function.
Substituting this into Eq. (
S23
), we obtain

\displaystyle\delta\langle\hat{O}(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{i\hbar}\int_{-\infty}^{t}dt^{\prime}\,f(t^{\prime})\left\langle\left[\hat{O}_{H}(t),\hat{A}_{H}(t^{\prime})\right]\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dt^{\prime}\,\chi(t,t^{\prime})\,f(t^{\prime}),

(S25)

where we introduced the generalized susceptibility

\displaystyle\chi(t,t^{\prime})=\frac{1}{i\hbar}\,\Theta(t-t^{\prime})\left\langle\left[\hat{O}_{H}(t),\hat{A}_{H}(t^{\prime})\right]\right\rangle.

(S26)

The step function
\Theta(t-t^{\prime})
explicitly enforces causality:
the response at time
t
depends only on the perturbation applied at earlier times
t^{\prime}<t
. Since the susceptibility depends only on the time difference
t-t^{\prime}
,
we write

\displaystyle\chi(t,t^{\prime})=\chi^{R}(t-t^{\prime}),

(S27)

where superscript
R
denotes the retarded response. Thus the linear response takes the convolution form of Kubo formula

\displaystyle\delta\langle\hat{O}(t)\rangle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dt^{\prime}\,\chi^{R}(t-t^{\prime})\,f(t^{\prime}).

(S28)

We now Fourier transform Eq. (
S28
).
Using the convention

\displaystyle\chi^{R}(t-t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\,e^{-i\omega(t-t^{\prime})}\,\chi^{R}(\omega),

(S29)

\displaystyle f(\omega)

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dt^{\prime}\,e^{i\omega t^{\prime}}f(t^{\prime}),

(S30)

we have

\displaystyle\delta\langle\hat{O}(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\,e^{-i\omega t}\,\chi^{R}(\omega)\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dt^{\prime}\,e^{i\omega t^{\prime}}f(t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\,e^{-i\omega t}\,\chi^{R}(\omega)\,f(\omega),

(S31)

and thus

\displaystyle\delta\langle\hat{O}(\omega)\rangle=\chi^{R}(\omega)\,f(\omega).

(S32)

Hence the Fourier transform of the retarded susceptibility is

\displaystyle\chi^{R}(\omega)=\frac{\delta\langle\hat{O}(\omega)\rangle}{f(\omega)}.

(S33)

Finally, the frequency-domain retarded susceptibility can be written as

\displaystyle\chi^{R}(\omega)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dt\,e^{i\omega t}\,\chi^{R}(t)=\frac{1}{i\hbar}\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\,e^{i(\omega+i0^{+})t}\left\langle\left[\hat{O}_{H}(t),\hat{A}_{H}(0)\right]\right\rangle,

(S34)

where the infinitesimal
i0^{+}
ensures convergence and selects the retarded boundary condition, i.e., the response depends only on the perturbation applied at earlier times.

I.2
Optical conductivity from linear response theory

We now apply the general formalism of linear response theory shown in Sec.
I.1
to the optical conductivity.
In the following, we set
\hbar=1
for simplicity.
We also assume that the electron charge and the lattice spacing are set to unity unless otherwise stated.

We present the derivation for a one-dimensional lattice model.
The generalization to higher dimensions is straightforward. We start from the Hamiltonian

\displaystyle\hat{H}=\hat{K}+\hat{H}_{\rm int},

(S35)

where
\hat{H}_{\rm int}
denotes the part of density-density interaction and the kinetic-energy term is

\displaystyle\hat{K}=-t_{h}\sum_{\sigma=\{\uparrow,\downarrow\}}\sum_{l=0}^{N-1}\left(c^{\dagger}_{l+1,\sigma}c_{l,\sigma}+c^{\dagger}_{l,\sigma}c_{l+1,\sigma}\right),

(S36)

where
c^{\dagger}_{l,\sigma}
and
c_{l,\sigma}
are the creation and annihilation operators, respectively, for a fermion with spin
\sigma
at site
l
,
t_{h}
is the hopping strength (we do not use
t
here in order to avoid confusion with the time variable
t
),
N
is the system size, i.e., the number of sites. We impose periodic boundary conditions (PBCs) throughout the work.

To derive the optical conductivity, we consider an external time-dependent vector potential projected onto the hopping direction
A(l,t)
(
l
is the site index and
t
denotes time)
coupled to the system through the Peierls substitution
Peierls 1933
. The hopping term is modified according to

\displaystyle c^{\dagger}_{l+1,\sigma}c_{l,\sigma}\;\to\;e^{+iA(l,t)}c^{\dagger}_{l+1,\sigma}c_{l,\sigma},

(S37)

\displaystyle c^{\dagger}_{l,\sigma}c_{l+1,\sigma}\;\to\;e^{-iA(l,t)}c^{\dagger}_{l,\sigma}c_{l+1,\sigma}.

(S38)

Thus the Hamiltonian becomes

\displaystyle\hat{H}[A]=-t_{h}\sum_{\sigma=\{\uparrow,\downarrow\}}\sum_{l=0}^{N-1}\left(e^{+iA(l,t)}c^{\dagger}_{l+1,\sigma}c_{l,\sigma}+e^{-iA(l,t)}c^{\dagger}_{l,\sigma}c_{l+1,\sigma}\right)+\hat{H}_{\rm int},

(S39)

where the density-density interaction part
\hat{H}_{\rm int}
remains unchanged.

We are interested in the linear response to a weak external field and thus expand Eq. (
S39
) to the second order in
A(l,t)
(to get the total current operator in Eq. (
S45
)).
Using

\displaystyle e^{\pm iA(l,t)}=1\pm iA(l,t)-\frac{1}{2}A^{2}(l,t)+\mathcal{O}(A^{3}),

(S40)

we obtain

\displaystyle\hat{H}[A]=\hat{H}-\sum_{l=0}^{N-1}\hat{J}_{l}\,A(l,t)-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{l=0}^{N-1}\hat{K}_{l}\,A^{2}(l,t)+\mathcal{O}(A^{3}),

(S41)

where we have introduced the local paramagnetic current operator

\displaystyle\hat{J}_{l}=it_{h}\sum_{\sigma=\{\uparrow,\downarrow\}}\left(\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{l+1,\sigma}\hat{c}_{l,\sigma}-\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{l,\sigma}\hat{c}_{l+1,\sigma}\right),

(S42)

and the local kinetic operator

\displaystyle\hat{K}_{l}=-t_{h}\sum_{\sigma=\{\uparrow,\downarrow\}}\left(\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{l+1,\sigma}\hat{c}_{l,\sigma}+\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{l,\sigma}\hat{c}_{l+1,\sigma}\right).

(S43)

We note that
\sum_{l}\hat{K}_{l}=\hat{K}
, namely the sum of the local kinetic operators
is precisely the kinetic-energy operator appearing in Eq. (
S36
).

The total current operator at site
l
is given by

\displaystyle\hat{J}_{\rm tot}(l,t)\equiv-\frac{\delta\hat{H}[A]}{\delta A(l,t)}=\hat{J}_{l}+\hat{K}_{l}\,A(l,t)+\mathcal{O}(A^{2}),

(S44)

and thus the total current operator is given by

\displaystyle\hat{J}_{\rm tot}(t)=\sum_{l=0}^{N-1}\hat{J}_{l}+\sum_{l=0}^{N-1}\hat{K}_{l}\,A(l,t)=\hat{J}+\hat{J}_{\mathrm{dia}}(t),

(S45)

where we have neglected higher-order terms in the external field. We note that

\displaystyle\hat{J}=\sum_{l=0}^{N-1}\hat{J}_{l}

(S46)

is the paramagnetic current operator and

\displaystyle\hat{J}_{\mathrm{dia}}(t)=\sum_{l=0}^{N-1}\hat{K}_{l}\,A(l,t)

(S47)

is the diamagnetic part. Therefore, to linear order in the external field, the total current operator consists of two contributions: the usual paramagnetic term
\hat{J}
which is already present at
A=0
; and diamagnetic term
\hat{J}_{\mathrm{dia}}(t)
, which arises from the explicit dependence of the Hamiltonian on the vector potential.

According to Eq. (
S41
), the time-dependent perturbation to the leading order in the vector potential is

\displaystyle\hat{V}(t)=-\sum_{l=0}^{N-1}\hat{J}_{l}\,A(l,t).

(S48)

Next, we derive the expectation value of the total current operator
\langle\hat{J}_{\rm tot}(t)\rangle
in Eq. (
S45
) in the presence of the time-dependent perturbation
\hat{V}(t)
in Eq. (
S48
) within linear response theory, and then derive the corresponding expression for conductivity. For simplicity, in the following, we use
\sum_{l}
as the shorthand of
\sum_{l=0}^{N-1}
.

I.2.1
Paramagnetic part

We first consider the paramagnetic part. We set the paramagnetic current operator
\hat{J}
in Eq. (
S46
) as the operator
\hat{O}
in Eq. (
S23
). We have

\displaystyle\delta\langle\hat{J}(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{i}\int_{-\infty}^{t}dt^{\prime}\,\left\langle\left[\hat{J}_{H}(t),\hat{V}_{H}(t^{\prime})\right]\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{i}\int_{-\infty}^{t}dt^{\prime}\,\left\langle\left[\sum_{l}\hat{J}_{l,H}(t),-\sum_{l^{\prime}}\hat{J}_{l^{\prime},H}(t^{\prime})A(l^{\prime},t^{\prime})\right]\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{i}\sum_{l,l^{\prime}}\int_{-\infty}^{t}dt^{\prime}\,\left\langle\left[\hat{J}_{l,H}(t),\hat{J}_{l^{\prime},H}(t^{\prime})\right]\right\rangle A(l^{\prime},t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=i\sum_{l,l^{\prime}}\int_{-\infty}^{t}dt^{\prime}\,\left\langle\left[\hat{J}_{l,H}(t),\hat{J}_{l^{\prime},H}(t^{\prime})\right]\right\rangle A(l^{\prime},t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=i\sum_{l,l^{\prime}}\int_{-\infty}^{t}dt^{\prime}\,\left\langle\left[\hat{J}_{l}(t),\hat{J}_{l^{\prime}}(t^{\prime})\right]\right\rangle A(l^{\prime},t^{\prime}),

(S49)

where
\hat{J}_{H}(t)=e^{i\hat{H}t}\hat{J}e^{-i\hat{H}t}
and
\hat{J}_{l,H}(t)=e^{i\hat{H}t}\hat{J}_{l}e^{-i\hat{H}t}
. Note that
A(l^{\prime},t^{\prime})
is a time-dependent function rather than an operator. In the last equality, we have omitted the subscript
H
since
\hat{J}_{l}(t)=\hat{J}_{l,H}(t)
.

We now Fourier transform the above expression to momentum space.
Starting from the response of the local current operator

\displaystyle\delta\langle\hat{J}_{l}(t)\rangle=i\sum_{l^{\prime}}\int_{-\infty}^{t}dt^{\prime}\,\left\langle\left[\hat{J}_{l}(t),\hat{J}_{l^{\prime}}(t^{\prime})\right]\right\rangle A(l^{\prime},t^{\prime}),

(S50)

we define the finite-momentum current operator as

\displaystyle\hat{J}(q,t)=\sum_{l}e^{-iql}\hat{J}_{l}(t),

(S51)

and thus

\displaystyle\delta\langle\hat{J}(q,t)\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{l}e^{-iql}\delta\langle\hat{J}_{l}(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=i\sum_{l,l^{\prime}}\int_{-\infty}^{t}dt^{\prime}\,e^{-iql}\left\langle\left[\hat{J}_{l}(t),\hat{J}_{l^{\prime}}(t^{\prime})\right]\right\rangle A(l^{\prime},t^{\prime}).

(S52)

Substituting

\displaystyle A(l^{\prime},t^{\prime})=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{q^{\prime}}e^{iq^{\prime}l^{\prime}}A(q^{\prime},t^{\prime}),

(S53)

we have

\displaystyle\delta\langle\hat{J}(q,t)\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{N}\sum_{q^{\prime}}\int_{-\infty}^{t}dt^{\prime}\,\sum_{l,l^{\prime}}e^{-iql}e^{iq^{\prime}l^{\prime}}\left\langle\left[\hat{J}_{l}(t),\hat{J}_{l^{\prime}}(t^{\prime})\right]\right\rangle A(q^{\prime},t^{\prime}).

(S54)

Using the definition

\displaystyle\hat{J}(q,t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l}e^{-iql}\hat{J}_{l}(t),

(S55)

\displaystyle\hat{J}(-q^{\prime},t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{l^{\prime}}e^{iq^{\prime}l^{\prime}}\hat{J}_{l^{\prime}}(t^{\prime}),

(S56)

Eq. (
S54
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\delta\langle\hat{J}(q,t)\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{N}\sum_{q^{\prime}}\int_{-\infty}^{t}dt^{\prime}\,\left\langle\left[\hat{J}(q,t),\hat{J}(-q^{\prime},t^{\prime})\right]\right\rangle A(q^{\prime},t^{\prime}),

(S57)

in which by translation invariance, only the contribution with
q^{\prime}=q
survives.
To see why only the contribution with
q^{\prime}=q
survives, we define

\displaystyle C_{q,q^{\prime}}(t,t^{\prime})\equiv\left\langle\left[\hat{J}(q,t),\hat{J}(-q^{\prime},t^{\prime})\right]\right\rangle=\text{Tr}\ \!\left(\hat{\rho}\left[\hat{J}(q,t),\hat{J}(-q^{\prime},t^{\prime})\right]\right).

(S58)

Since the system is translationally invariant, the translation operator
\hat{T}_{x}
satisfies

\displaystyle[\hat{T}_{x},\hat{H}]=0.

(S59)

Therefore, the thermal density matrix
\hat{\rho}

also commutes with
\hat{T}_{x}
. On the other hand, the current operator in momentum space transforms under translation as

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{x}\,\hat{J}(q,t)\,\hat{T}_{x}^{-1}

\displaystyle=e^{iq}\hat{J}(q,t),

(S60)

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{x}\,\hat{J}(-q^{\prime},t^{\prime})\,\hat{T}_{x}^{-1}

\displaystyle=e^{-iq^{\prime}}\hat{J}(-q^{\prime},t^{\prime}).

(S61)

Using these relations, together with
[\hat{T}_{x},\hat{\rho}]=0
and the cyclic property of the trace,
we obtain

\displaystyle C_{q,q^{\prime}}(t,t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\text{Tr}\ \!\left(\hat{\rho}\left[\hat{T}_{x}\hat{J}(q,t)\hat{T}_{x}^{-1},\hat{T}_{x}\hat{J}(-q^{\prime},t^{\prime})\hat{T}_{x}^{-1}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=e^{i(q-q^{\prime})}\text{Tr}\ \!\left(\hat{\rho}\left[\hat{J}(q,t),\hat{J}(-q^{\prime},t^{\prime})\right]\right)

\displaystyle=e^{i(q-q^{\prime})}C_{q,q^{\prime}}(t,t^{\prime}).

(S62)

Hence

\displaystyle\bigl(1-e^{i(q-q^{\prime})}\bigr)C_{q,q^{\prime}}(t,t^{\prime})=0.

(S63)

Therefore,
C_{q,q^{\prime}}(t,t^{\prime})
can be nonzero only if

\displaystyle e^{i(q-q^{\prime})}=1,

(S64)

namely
q=q^{\prime}
modulo
2\pi
. As a result, in the linear-response expression only the contribution with
q^{\prime}=q
survives.

Therefore, Eq. (
S57
) becomes

\displaystyle\delta\langle\hat{J}(q,t)\rangle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dt^{\prime}\,\chi^{R}_{JJ}(q,t-t^{\prime})\,A(q,t^{\prime}),

(S65)

where the retarded current–current correlator is defined as

\displaystyle\chi^{R}_{JJ}(q,t-t^{\prime})=\frac{i}{N}\Theta(t-t^{\prime})\left\langle\left[\hat{J}(q,t),\hat{J}(-q,t^{\prime})\right]\right\rangle.

(S66)

where the correlator depends only on the time difference
t-t^{\prime}
.
Using the Fourier-transform convention

\displaystyle\chi^{R}_{JJ}(q,t-t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\,e^{-i\omega(t-t^{\prime})}\,\chi^{R}_{JJ}(q,\omega),

(S67)

\displaystyle A(q,\omega)

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dt\,e^{i\omega t}A(q,t),

(S68)

with inverse transform

\displaystyle A(q,t)=\int\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\,e^{-i\omega t}A(q,\omega),

(S69)

we obtain

\displaystyle\delta\langle\hat{J}(q,t)\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dt^{\prime}\,\chi^{R}_{JJ}(q,t-t^{\prime})A(q,t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\,e^{-i\omega t}\,\chi^{R}_{JJ}(q,\omega)\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dt^{\prime}\,e^{i\omega t^{\prime}}A(q,t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\,e^{-i\omega t}\,\chi^{R}_{JJ}(q,\omega)A(q,\omega).

(S70)

Therefore, in frequency space we arrive at

\displaystyle\delta\langle\hat{J}(q,\omega)\rangle=\chi^{R}_{JJ}(q,\omega)A(q,\omega),

(S71)

with

\displaystyle\chi^{R}_{JJ}(q,\omega)

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dt\,e^{i\omega t}\chi^{R}_{JJ}(q,t)

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{N}\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\,e^{i(\omega+i0^{+})t}\left\langle\left[\hat{J}(q,t),\hat{J}(-q,0)\right]\right\rangle.

(S72)

where the infinitesimal
i0^{+}
has been introduced to implement the retarded prescription; equivalently, it contributes a damping factor
e^{i(i0^{+})t}=e^{-0^{+}t}
, which ensures the convergence of the time integral for
t>0
.

I.2.2
Diamagnetic part

We now add back the diamagnetic contribution.
Since the diamagnetic part shown in Eq. (
S47
)
is already linear in the external field, within linear response theory
it is sufficient to evaluate its thermal-equilibrium expectation value

\displaystyle\langle\hat{J}_{\rm dia}(t)\rangle=\sum_{l=0}^{N-1}\langle\hat{K}_{l}\rangle A(l,t).

(S73)

Since

\displaystyle\sum_{l=0}^{N-1}\hat{K}_{l}A(l,t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=0}^{N-1}\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{q}e^{iql}\hat{K}(q)\right)\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{q^{\prime}}e^{iq^{\prime}l}A(q^{\prime},t)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{q,q^{\prime}}\hat{K}(q)A(q^{\prime},t)\sum_{l=0}^{N-1}e^{i(q+q^{\prime})l}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{q}\hat{K}(-q)A(q,t),

(S74)

the diamagnetic contribution to the current expectation value is

\displaystyle\langle\hat{J}_{\rm dia}(t)\rangle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{q}\langle\hat{K}(-q)\rangle A(q,t).

(S75)

We now Fourier transform with respect to time. Using

\displaystyle A(q,\omega)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dt\,e^{i\omega t}A(q,t),

(S76)

Eq. (
S75
) becomes

\displaystyle\langle\hat{J}_{\rm dia}(\omega)\rangle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{q}\langle\hat{K}(-q)\rangle A(q,\omega).

(S77)

Therefore, combining Eq. (
S71
) and Eq. (
S77
), the expectation value of the total current operator is

\displaystyle\langle\hat{J}_{\mathrm{tot}}(q,\omega)\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\hat{J}(q,\omega)\rangle+\delta\langle\hat{J}(q,\omega)\rangle+\langle\hat{J}_{\mathrm{dia}}(q,\omega)\rangle

\displaystyle=\left(\chi^{R}_{JJ}(q,\omega)+\frac{1}{N}\langle\hat{K}(-q)\rangle\right)A(q,\omega),

(S78)

where in the second equality we have used
\langle\hat{J}(q,\omega)\rangle=0
, i.e., the expectation value of paramagnetic current operator in thermal equilibrium is zero.

The frequency-dependent uniform electric conductivity
\sigma(\omega)
is given by the current response to an electric field with
q=0

\displaystyle E(q=0,\omega)=i\omega A(q=0,\omega).

(S79)

Moreover, since

\displaystyle\langle\hat{J}_{\rm tot}(q=0,\omega)\rangle=\sigma(\omega)\,E(q=0,\omega),

(S80)

we obtain the expression for conductivity

\displaystyle\sigma(\omega)

\displaystyle=\frac{\frac{1}{N}\langle\hat{K}\rangle+\chi^{R}_{JJ}(\omega)}{i(\omega+i0^{+})}

\displaystyle=i\frac{\frac{1}{N}\langle-\hat{K}\rangle-\chi^{R}_{JJ}(\omega)}{\omega+i0^{+}},

(S81)

where

\displaystyle\chi^{R}_{JJ}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{N}\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\,e^{i(\omega+i0^{+})t}\left\langle\left[\hat{J}(t),\hat{J}(0)\right]\right\rangle.

(S82)

I.3
Drude weight and regular part of the optical conductivity

In Sec.
I.2
, as shown in Eq. (
I.2.2
), we have derived the expression for conductivity
\sigma(\omega)
which is complex. Its real part characterizes the dissipative response of the system, namely the absorption of energy from the external electric field. For this reason, its real part is directly associated with charge transport and is the quantity measured in standard optical conductivity experiments
Basov et al. 2011
;
Kuzmenko 2005
. By contrast, the imaginary part describes the reactive or nondissipative response. It encodes the phase shift between the applied electric field and the induced current, and is related to the temporary storage rather than irreversible dissipation of electromagnetic energy
Basov et al. 2011
;
Kuzmenko 2005
. In the following, we focus on the real part of optical conductivity.

The real part of optical conductivity is usually decomposed as

\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\,\sigma(\omega,T)=2\pi D(T)\,\delta(\omega)+\sigma_{\mathrm{reg}}(\omega,T),

(S83)

where
2\pi D(T)\,\delta(\omega)
is the singular contribution at zero frequency, with
D(T)
the Drude weight, and
\sigma_{\mathrm{reg}}(\omega,T)
denotes the regular part at finite frequency
\omega>0
. Here “regular” means the part that does not contain a
\delta(\omega)
singularity at zero frequency. In the following, we present the expressions for
D(T)
and
\sigma_{\mathrm{reg}}(\omega,T)
.

Using the distribution identity

\frac{1}{\omega+i0^{+}}=\mathcal{P}\frac{1}{\omega}-i\pi\delta(\omega),

(S84)

we rewrite Eq. (
I.2.2
)
as

\sigma(\omega,T)=i\Big(\frac{\langle-\hat{K}\rangle}{N}-\chi^{R}_{JJ}(\omega)\Big)\,\mathcal{P}\frac{1}{\omega}+\pi\Big(\frac{\langle-\hat{K}\rangle}{N}-\chi^{R}_{JJ}(\omega)\Big)\,\delta(\omega).

(S85)

Since
f(\omega)\delta(\omega)=f(0)\delta(\omega)
in the sense of distributions, only the value at
\omega=0

enters the
\delta
-peak. Taking the real part
of Eq. (
S85
) yields

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\,\sigma(\omega,T)

\displaystyle=\pi\Big[\frac{\langle-\hat{K}\rangle}{N}-\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\,\chi^{R}_{JJ}(0)\Big]\delta(\omega)+\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\!\left[i\Big(\frac{\langle-\hat{K}\rangle}{N}-\chi^{R}_{JJ}(\omega)\Big)\,\mathcal{P}\frac{1}{\omega}\right]

\displaystyle=\pi\Big[\frac{\langle-\hat{K}\rangle}{N}-\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\,\chi^{R}_{JJ}(0)\Big]\delta(\omega)-\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\!\left[i\chi^{R}_{JJ}(\omega)\,\mathcal{P}\frac{1}{\omega}\right],

(S86)

where we used
\langle-\hat{K}\rangle\in\mathbb{R}
and hence

\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\!\left[\frac{i}{N}\langle-\hat{K}\rangle\,\mathcal{P}\frac{1}{\omega}\right]=0
.
Writing
\chi^{R}_{JJ}(\omega)=\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\chi^{R}_{JJ}(\omega)+i\,\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\chi^{R}_{JJ}(\omega)
, we obtain

\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\,\sigma(\omega,T)=\pi\Big[\frac{\langle-\hat{K}\rangle}{N}-\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\,\chi^{R}_{JJ}(0)\Big]\delta(\omega)+\,\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\chi^{R}_{JJ}(\omega)\,\mathcal{P}\frac{1}{\omega}.

(S87)

For
\omega\neq 0
, the
\delta(\omega)
term vanishes and
\mathcal{P}(1/\omega)=1/\omega
, so that

\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\,\sigma(\omega,T)=\pi\Big[\frac{\langle-\hat{K}\rangle}{N}-\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\,\chi^{R}_{JJ}(0)\Big]\delta(\omega)+\frac{1}{\omega}\,\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\chi^{R}_{JJ}(\omega)\,.

(S88)

Comparing Eqs. (
S88
) and (
S83
)
gives

D(T)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\langle-\hat{K}\rangle}{N}-\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\,\chi^{R}_{JJ}(0)\right),

(S89)

and

\sigma_{\mathrm{reg}}(\omega,T)=\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\,\chi^{R}_{JJ}(\omega)}{\omega},\qquad(\omega\neq 0).

(S90)

Note that the expression of regular does not apply at strictly zero frequency.
The DC conductivity at
\omega=0
is given by

\displaystyle\sigma_{\mathrm{DC}}(T)=2\pi D(T)\delta(\omega)+\lim_{\omega\to 0}\sigma_{\mathrm{reg}}(\omega,T).

(S91)

I.4
Drude weight and regular part in the Lehmann representation

Next, we provide the expression for Drude weight and regular part in the Lehmann representation.

Let
|n\rangle
denote the eigenstates of
\hat{H}
with eigenenergies
E_{n}
, i.e.,

\hat{H}|n\rangle=E_{n}|n\rangle
, and define
J_{mn}\equiv\langle m|\hat{J}|n\rangle
.
Inserting a complete set of eigenstates yields

\displaystyle\langle[\hat{J}(t),\hat{J}(0)]\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z}\sum_{n}e^{-\beta E_{n}}\langle n|[\hat{J}(t),\hat{J}(0)]|n\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z}\sum_{n,m}e^{-\beta E_{n}}\Big(\langle n|\hat{J}(t)|m\rangle\langle m|\hat{J}(0)|n\rangle-\langle n|\hat{J}(0)|m\rangle\langle m|\hat{J}(t)|n\rangle\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z}\sum_{n,m}e^{-\beta E_{n}}\Big(e^{i(E_{n}-E_{m})t}-e^{-i(E_{n}-E_{m})t}\Big)|J_{mn}|^{2}.

(S92)

Using

\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\,e^{i(\omega-\Delta+i0^{+})t}=\frac{i}{\omega-\Delta+i0^{+}},

(S93)

we obtain the Lehmann representation of the retarded current–current correlator defined in Eq. (
S82
)

\chi^{R}_{JJ}(\omega)=-\frac{1}{NZ}\sum_{n,m}\frac{e^{-\beta E_{n}}-e^{-\beta E_{m}}}{\omega+i0^{+}-(E_{m}-E_{n})}\,|J_{mn}|^{2}.

(S94)

Using
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\,(x+i0^{+})^{-1}=-\pi\delta(x)
, Eq. (
S94
) gives

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\,\chi^{R}_{JJ}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{NZ}\sum_{n,m}\big(e^{-\beta E_{n}}-e^{-\beta E_{m}}\big)\,|J_{mn}|^{2}\,\delta\!\big(\omega-(E_{m}-E_{n})\big)

\displaystyle=\pi\,\frac{1-e^{-\beta\omega}}{NZ}\sum_{n,m}e^{-\beta E_{n}}\,|J_{mn}|^{2}\,\delta(E_{n}+\omega-E_{m}),

(S95)

where in the second line we used the delta-function constraint
E_{m}=E_{n}+\omega
.

Substituting Eq. (
S95
) into Eq. (
S90
) yields

\sigma_{\mathrm{reg}}(\omega,T)=\pi\,\frac{1-e^{-\beta\omega}}{\omega ZN}\sum_{n,m}e^{-\beta E_{n}}\,|J_{mn}|^{2}\,\delta(E_{n}+\omega-E_{m}),\qquad(\omega\neq 0).

(S96)

From Eq. (
S94
), the real part at
\omega=0
is understood as a principal value which makes it explicit that only the terms with
E_{m}\neq E_{n}
contribute

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\,\chi^{R}_{JJ}(0)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{NZ}\sum_{n,m}\big(e^{-\beta E_{n}}-e^{-\beta E_{m}}\big)\,\mathcal{P}\frac{1}{-(E_{m}-E_{n})}\,|J_{mn}|^{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{NZ}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m,n\\
E_{m}\neq E_{n}\end{subarray}}e^{-\beta E_{n}}\frac{|J_{mn}|^{2}}{E_{n}-E_{m}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{NZ}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m,n\\
E_{m}\neq E_{n}\end{subarray}}e^{-\beta E_{n}}\frac{|J_{mn}|^{2}}{E_{m}-E_{n}}.

(S97)

Substituting Eq. (
S97
) into Eq. (
S89
), we obtain

D(T)=\frac{1}{N}\left[\frac{1}{2}\,\langle-\hat{K}\rangle-\frac{1}{Z}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m,n\\
E_{m}\neq E_{n}\end{subarray}}e^{-\beta E_{n}}\frac{|J_{mn}|^{2}}{E_{m}-E_{n}}\right].

(S98)

Therefore, we have proved Eq. (
4
) and Eq. (
5
) in the main text.

II
Model and observables

In this section, we introduce the one-dimensional Hubbard model in the infinite-interaction limit and present the definitions of the current operator, the optical conductivity, and the specific heat. Finally, we provide a summary of the analytical results obtained in the subsequent sections.

II.1
1D Hubbard model

The Hamiltonian of the one-dimensional (1D) Hubbard model is

\displaystyle\hat{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-t\sum_{\sigma=\{\uparrow,\downarrow\}}\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}(c^{\dagger}_{j+1,\sigma}c_{j,\sigma}+c^{\dagger}_{j,\sigma}c_{j+1,\sigma})+U\sum_{j}n_{j,\uparrow}n_{j,\downarrow},

(S99)

where
c^{\dagger}_{j,\sigma}
and
c_{j,\sigma}
denote the creation and annihilation operators of a fermion with spin
\sigma
at site
j
respectively,
n_{j,\sigma}=c^{\dagger}_{j,\sigma}c_{j,\sigma}
is the corresponding density operator. Here,
t
and
U
denote the hopping amplitude and the on-site interaction strength, respectively.

The model considered in the main text is the 1D Hubbard model in the infinite-interaction limit, which is equivalent to the
t
–
J
model with
J=0
. The Hamiltonian reads

\displaystyle\hat{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-t\sum_{\sigma=\{\uparrow,\downarrow\}}\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}(\tilde{c}^{\dagger}_{j+1,\sigma}\tilde{c}_{j,\sigma}+\tilde{c}^{\dagger}_{j,\sigma}\tilde{c}_{j+1,\sigma}).

(S100)

Here
\tilde{c}^{\dagger}_{j,\sigma}=(1-n_{j,\bar{\sigma}})\,c^{\dagger}_{j,\sigma}
denotes the projected creation operator of a fermion with spin
\sigma
at site
j
in the subspace without double occupancy, and
n_{j,\bar{\sigma}}=c^{\dagger}_{j,\bar{\sigma}}c_{j,\bar{\sigma}}
is the corresponding density operator of fermion with opposite spin
\bar{\sigma}
. The hopping amplitude
t
sets the energy scale and is fixed to
t=1
throughout. Periodic boundary conditions (PBCs) are imposed, such that
\tilde{c}^{\dagger}_{N,\sigma}\equiv\tilde{c}^{\dagger}_{0,\sigma}
, where
N
denotes the system size, i.e., the total number of lattice sites.

The Hamiltonian in Eq. (
S100
) preserves translational symmetry as well as a
U(1)\times U(1)
symmetry. The former corresponds to lattice translation invariance, while the latter reflects the conservation of total charge (
\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}(n_{j,\uparrow}+n_{j,\downarrow})
) and total magnetization (
\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}(n_{j,\uparrow}-n_{j,\downarrow})/2
).

II.2
Current operator and proof of
[\hat{J},\hat{H}]=0

For the 1D Hubbard model in the infinite-interaction limit defined in Eq. (
S100
), the current operator
\hat{J}
is

\displaystyle\hat{J}=i\sum_{\sigma=\{\uparrow,\downarrow\}}\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}(\tilde{c}^{\dagger}_{j+1,\sigma}\tilde{c}_{j,\sigma}-\tilde{c}^{\dagger}_{j,\sigma}\tilde{c}_{j+1,\sigma}).

(S101)

Note we have set
t=1
. We find the current operator
\hat{J}
commutes with the Hamiltonian
\hat{H}
, i.e.,
[\hat{J},\hat{H}]=0
, whereas in the conventional 1D Hubbard model one generally has
[\hat{J},\hat{H}]\neq 0
. We now present an explicit proof that
[\hat{J},\hat{H}]=0
in the infinite-interaction limit.
In our case, with doped holes, double occupancy is still forbidden, and we work directly in the no-double-occupancy subspace when deriving the eigenstates. Therefore,
(1-n_{j,\bar{\sigma}})
in
\tilde{c}^{\dagger}_{j,\sigma}
becomes redundant. Here, however, to verify
[\hat{J},\hat{H}]=0
, we keep it for completeness.
We define the local current operator

\hat{J}_{j,j+1,\sigma}=i\bigl(\tilde{c}^{\dagger}_{j+1,\sigma}\tilde{c}_{j,\sigma}-\tilde{c}^{\dagger}_{j,\sigma}\tilde{c}_{j+1,\sigma}\bigr)=i(1-n_{j+1,\bar{\sigma}})\bigl(c^{\dagger}_{j+1,\sigma}c_{j,\sigma}-c^{\dagger}_{j,\sigma}c_{j+1,\sigma}\bigr)(1-n_{j,\bar{\sigma}}),

(S102)

such that
\hat{J}=\sum_{j,\sigma}\hat{J}_{j,j+1,\sigma}
. We first evaluate the commutator
[\hat{J}_{j,j+1,\sigma},\hat{H}]
, which can be decomposed as

[\hat{J}_{j,j+1,\sigma},\hat{H}]=[\hat{J}_{j,j+1,\sigma},\hat{H}_{\sigma}]+[\hat{J}_{j,j+1,\sigma},\hat{H}_{\bar{\sigma}}],

(S103)

where
\hat{H}_{\sigma}
and
\hat{H}_{\bar{\sigma}}
denote the hopping terms for spin
\sigma
and opposite spin
\bar{\sigma}
, respectively.

For the same-spin contribution
[\hat{J}_{j,j+1,\sigma},\hat{H}_{\sigma}]
, only the hopping terms in
\hat{H}_{\sigma}
involving sites
j-1
,
j
,
j+1
, and
j+2
contribute. A straightforward calculation yields

\displaystyle[\hat{J}_{j,j+1,\sigma},\hat{H}_{\sigma}]

\displaystyle=\Bigl(-i(1-n_{j+2,\bar{\sigma}})(1-n_{j+1,\bar{\sigma}})(1-n_{j,\bar{\sigma}})(-c^{\dagger}_{j+2,\sigma}c_{j,\sigma})

\displaystyle\quad-i(1-n_{j+1,\bar{\sigma}})(1-n_{j,\bar{\sigma}})(n_{j+1,\sigma}-n_{j,\sigma})

\displaystyle\quad-i(1-n_{j+1,\bar{\sigma}})(1-n_{j,\bar{\sigma}})(1-n_{j-1,\bar{\sigma}})c^{\dagger}_{j+1,\sigma}c_{j-1,\sigma}\Bigr)+\text{H.c.}

(S104)

Summing over
j
and
\sigma
, these terms cancel pairwise, leading to

\sum_{j,\sigma}[\hat{J}_{j,j+1,\sigma},\hat{H}_{\sigma}]=0.

(S105)

For the opposite-spin contribution
[\hat{J}_{j,j+1,\sigma},\hat{H}_{\bar{\sigma}}]
, one finds

\displaystyle[\hat{J}_{j,j+1,\sigma},\hat{H}_{\bar{\sigma}}]

\displaystyle=\Bigl(-i(1-n_{j,\bar{\sigma}})(1-n_{j+2,\sigma})c^{\dagger}_{j+1,\sigma}c_{j,\sigma}c^{\dagger}_{j+2,\bar{\sigma}}c_{j+1,\bar{\sigma}}

\displaystyle\quad+i(1-n_{j+1,\bar{\sigma}})(1-n_{j-1,\sigma})c^{\dagger}_{j+1,\sigma}c_{j,\sigma}c^{\dagger}_{j,\bar{\sigma}}c_{j-1,\bar{\sigma}}\Bigr)+\text{H.c.}

(S106)

Upon summation over
j
and
\sigma
,

\displaystyle\sum_{j,\sigma}[\hat{J}_{j,j+1,\sigma},\hat{H}_{\bar{\sigma}}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j,\sigma}\left(-i(1-n_{j,\bar{\sigma}})(1-n_{j+2,\sigma})c^{\dagger}_{j+1,\sigma}c_{j,\sigma}c^{\dagger}_{j+2,\bar{\sigma}}c_{j+1,\bar{\sigma}}\right.

(S107)

\displaystyle\left.+i(1-n_{j+1,\bar{\sigma}})(1-n_{j-1,\sigma})c^{\dagger}_{j+1,\sigma}c_{j,\sigma}c^{\dagger}_{j,\bar{\sigma}}c_{j-1,\bar{\sigma}})\right)+\text{H.c.}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{j,\sigma}-i(1-n_{j,\bar{\sigma}})(1-n_{j+2,\sigma})c^{\dagger}_{j+1,\sigma}c_{j,\sigma}c^{\dagger}_{j+2,\bar{\sigma}}c_{j+1,\bar{\sigma}}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\sum_{j,\sigma}i(1-n_{j+1,\sigma})(1-n_{j-1,\bar{\sigma}})c^{\dagger}_{j+1,\bar{\sigma}}c_{j,\bar{\sigma}}c^{\dagger}_{j,\sigma}c_{j-1,\sigma})\right)+\text{H.c.}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{j,\sigma}-i(1-n_{j,\bar{\sigma}})(1-n_{j+2,\sigma})c^{\dagger}_{j+1,\sigma}c_{j,\sigma}c^{\dagger}_{j+2,\bar{\sigma}}c_{j+1,\bar{\sigma}}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\sum_{j,\sigma}i(1-n_{j+2,\sigma})(1-n_{j,\bar{\sigma}})c^{\dagger}_{j+2,\bar{\sigma}}c_{j+1,\bar{\sigma}}c^{\dagger}_{j+1,\sigma}c_{j,\sigma})\right)+\text{H.c.},

the contributions again cancel pairwise, yielding

\sum_{j,\sigma}[\hat{J}_{j,j+1,\sigma},\hat{H}_{\bar{\sigma}}]=0.

(S108)

Combining the two parts, we obtain

[\hat{J},\hat{H}]=0.

(S109)

The Hamiltonian and the current operator hence share common eigenstates.

II.3
Optical conductivity

We have obtained the expressions for Drude weight (see Eq. (
S98
)) and regular part (see Eq. (
S96
)). Now we apply these results to the present model.

Let
|n\rangle
denote an eigenstate of the Hamiltonian
\hat{H}
with eigenenergy
E_{n}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\hat{H}|n\rangle=E_{n}|n\rangle.

(S110)

If
[\hat{J},\hat{H}]=0
, then

\displaystyle\langle n|[\hat{J},\hat{H}]|m\rangle

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad\langle n|(\hat{J}\hat{H}-\hat{H}\hat{J})|m\rangle

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad(E_{m}-E_{n})\langle n|\hat{J}|m\rangle

\displaystyle=0.

(S111)

Therefore, when
E_{m}\neq E_{n}
, we have

\displaystyle\langle n|\hat{J}|m\rangle=0.

(S112)

Now consider the case in which
\hat{H}
has a degenerate eigenspace. Let

|E,1\rangle,\cdots,|E,g_{E}\rangle
be an orthonormal basis of the
g_{E}
-fold degenerate eigenspace with eigenenergy
E
. Since
[\hat{J},\hat{H}]=0
, for each
i=1,\cdots,g_{E}
we have

\displaystyle\hat{H}\hat{J}|E,i\rangle=\hat{J}\hat{H}|E,i\rangle=E\,\hat{J}|E,i\rangle.

(S113)

Therefore,
\hat{J}|E,i\rangle
also belongs to the same eigenspace with energy
E
. We may thus restrict
\hat{J}
to this
g_{E}
-dimensional subspace and define the corresponding matrix

\displaystyle J^{(E)}_{ij}\equiv\langle E,i|\hat{J}|E,j\rangle,\qquad i,j=1,\cdots,g_{E}.

(S114)

Since
\hat{J}
is Hermitian, the matrix
J^{(E)}
is also Hermitian. Hence there exists a unitary matrix
U^{(E)}
such that

\displaystyle\bigl(U^{(E)}\bigr)^{\dagger}J^{(E)}U^{(E)}=\mathrm{diag}\!\bigl(j_{E,1},j_{E,2},\cdots,j_{E,g_{E}}\bigr).

(S115)

We then define a new basis within the same degenerate eigenspace by

\displaystyle|E,\gamma\rangle^{\prime}\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{g_{E}}|E,i\rangle\,U^{(E)}_{i\gamma},\qquad\gamma=1,\cdots,g_{E}.

(S116)

Because this transformation is performed entirely within the degenerate subspace, the new basis states are still eigenstates of
\hat{H}
with the same energy
E
:

\displaystyle\hat{H}|E,\gamma\rangle^{\prime}=E|E,\gamma\rangle^{\prime}.

(S117)

Moreover, in this new basis, the matrix elements of
\hat{J}
are

\displaystyle\langle E,\gamma_{1}|^{\prime}\hat{J}|E,\gamma_{2}\rangle^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}\bigl(U^{(E)}_{i\gamma_{1}}\bigr)^{*}\langle E,i|\hat{J}|E,j\rangle U^{(E)}_{j\gamma_{2}}

\displaystyle=\left[\bigl(U^{(E)}\bigr)^{\dagger}J^{(E)}U^{(E)}\right]_{\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}}

\displaystyle=j_{E,\gamma_{1}}\,\delta_{\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}}.

(S118)

Therefore, within each degenerate eigenspace of
\hat{H}
, one can always choose a basis in which
\hat{J}
is diagonal.

Combining all energy eigenspaces, we conclude that if
[\hat{H},\hat{J}]=0
, then one can always choose a complete orthonormal basis of simultaneous eigenstates of
\hat{H}
and
\hat{J}
. In such a basis,

\displaystyle\langle m|\hat{J}|n\rangle=0,\qquad m\neq n.

(S119)

Equivalently, all off-diagonal matrix elements of
\hat{J}
vanish in the common eigenbasis.

Therefore, for the present model, the Drude weight (see Eq. (
S98
)) simplifies to

\displaystyle D(T)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2N}\,\langle-\hat{K}\rangle=\frac{1}{2N}\langle-\hat{H}\rangle,

(S120)

where we have used the fact that in the present model the kinetic-energy operator
\hat{K}
coincides with the Hamiltonian
\hat{H}
, and the regular part (see Eq. (
S96
)) is

\displaystyle\sigma_{\mathrm{reg}}(\omega,T)

\displaystyle=\pi\frac{1-e^{-\beta\omega}}{\omega ZN}\sum_{n}e^{-\beta E_{n}}\left|J_{nn}\right|^{2}\delta(\omega).

(S121)

The finite-frequency regular part vanishes and thus

\displaystyle\lim_{\omega\to 0}\sigma_{\mathrm{reg}}(\omega)=0.

(S122)

Consequently, the dc conductivity consists solely of the Drude peak.

Moreover, although the expression for the regular part is not strictly applicable at zero frequency, it is related to the Drude weight
Shastry 2006
;
Mukerjee and Shastry 2008
.
By formally setting
\omega=0
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sigma_{\mathrm{diff}}(\omega,T)

\displaystyle=\pi\frac{\beta}{ZN}\sum_{n}e^{-\beta E_{n}}\left|J_{nn}\right|^{2}\delta(\omega),

(S123)

where we have used
\lim_{\omega\to 0}\frac{1-e^{-\beta\omega}}{\omega}=\beta
.
Here we denote it by
\sigma_{\mathrm{diff}}(\omega,T)
because it corresponds to the difference between the Drude weight and the Meissner stiffness, and to distinguish it from the regular part, which only applies at
\omega>0
.
As shown in Refs.
Shastry 2006
;
Mukerjee and Shastry 2008
, we have the following equality

\displaystyle 2\pi D(T)\delta(\omega)-2\pi D_{m}(T)\delta(\omega)=\sigma_{\mathrm{diff}}(\omega,T),

(S124)

where
D_{m}(T)
is the Meissner stiffness
Shastry 2006
;
Mukerjee and Shastry 2008
.
D_{m}(T)=0
indicates that the system is not a superconductor. In Ref.
Mukerjee and Shastry 2008
, the authors have numerically demonstrated that
D_{m}(T)=0
in one dimensional spinless fermion model.
In Secs.
III.1
and
III.2
, we have proven that for the present model
2\pi D(T)\delta(\omega)=\sigma_{\mathrm{diff}}(\omega,T)
, i.e.,
D_{m}(T)=0
, consistent with the fact that there is no superconductivity in one dimension.

II.4
Specific heat

In addition to the optical conductivity and resistivity, we also consider the specific heat, which is defined as

\displaystyle C_{V}(T)=\beta^{2}\left(\langle\hat{H}^{2}\rangle-\langle\hat{H}\rangle^{2}\right),

(S125)

where the thermal expectation value of an operator
\hat{O}
is given by

\langle\hat{O}\rangle=\frac{\mathrm{Tr}\left(e^{-\beta\hat{H}}\hat{O}\right)}{Z}=\frac{\sum_{n}e^{-\beta E_{n}}\langle n|\hat{O}|n\rangle}{Z},

(S126)

The specific heat is therefore determined by the energy fluctuations.

II.5
Summary of analytical results

We first summarize the energy spectrum
E
, eigenvalues
J
of the current operator
\hat{J}
with fixed number of holes in Table.
2
and
analytical expressions for Drude weight and specific heat with fixed number of holes in Table.
3
. Here,
n_{h}
and
n_{\downarrow}
denote the numbers of holes and spin-down fermions, respectively.

I_{l}(\cdot)
denotes the
l
-th modified Bessel function of the first kind. Detailed derivations are provided in the following sections.

In Sec.
V
, we have also obtained the analytical expressions for the Drude weight at fixed hole density in both the high-temperature and low-temperature limits (see Eq. (
S478
) and Eq. (
S538
)).

III
Explicit eigenstates and energy spectrum

In this section, we derive the explicit eigenstates and energy spectrum of the present model. We begin with the single-hole case (Sec.
III.1
), then proceed to the two-hole case (Sec.
III.2
), and finally generalize to the case with an arbitrary number of holes (Sec.
III.3
).

Since we work directly in the subspace without double occupancy, the projection is implicitly enforced, and for simplicity we omit the tilde notation in the following discussion, i.e.,
\tilde{c}\rightarrow c
.

III.1
Single-hole case

We first specify the convention for the Fock states. We use
n_{\downarrow}
to denote the number of spin-down fermions and
|\mathrm{vac}\rangle
to denote the vacuum state. We use the symbol
0
to denote a hole, and
\sigma=1,-1
to denote a spin-up or spin-down fermion, respectively.

A state with the hole at site
j
and
N-1
fermions occupying the remaining sites
j^{\prime}
with spin
\sigma_{j^{\prime}}
is written as

\displaystyle|\sigma_{0},\cdots,\sigma_{j-1},0,\sigma_{j+1},\cdots,\sigma_{N-1}\rangle=c^{\dagger}_{0,\sigma_{0}}\cdots c^{\dagger}_{j-1,\sigma_{j-1}}c^{\dagger}_{j+1,\sigma_{j+1}}\cdots c^{\dagger}_{N-1,\sigma_{N-1}}|\mathrm{vac}\rangle,

(S127)

where the fermion creation operators are arranged in ascending order of the site index when acting on the vacuum state. When
n_{\downarrow}=0
or
N-1
, the state can be denoted simply by
|j\rangle
where
j
is the position of the hole. When
0<n_{\downarrow}<N-1
, there are
N\binom{N-1}{n_{\downarrow}}
possible Fock states, and the state can be denoted by
|j,\alpha\rangle
: in the reduced
(N-1)
-site chain obtained by removing the site occupied by the hole, we label by site
0
the site immediately to the right of the hole in the original
N
-site chain. Let the sites occupied by the
n_{\downarrow}
spin-down fermions in the reduced chain be indexed by
j_{1}<j_{2}<\cdots<j_{n_{\downarrow}}
.
Then
\alpha
is given by

\displaystyle\alpha(j_{1},\ldots,j_{n_{\downarrow}})=\binom{N-1}{n_{\downarrow}}-1-\sum_{a=1}^{n_{\downarrow}}\binom{N-2-j_{a}}{\,n_{\downarrow}-a+1\,}.

(S128)

Here we adopt the convention that
\binom{m}{r}=0\ \text{if }m<r\text{ or }r<0
. When
n_{\downarrow}=1
, Eq. (
S128
) reduces to

\displaystyle\alpha(j_{1})

\displaystyle=\binom{N-1}{1}-1-\binom{N-2-j_{1}}{1}=j_{1},

(S129)

i.e.,
\alpha
is simply the index of the site occupied by the spin-down fermion in the reduced
(N-1)
-site chain. For example, the following state can be denoted by
|j,\alpha\rangle
with
j=0
and
\alpha=1
:

|0,1,-1,1,1\rangle=c^{\dagger}_{1,\uparrow}c^{\dagger}_{2,\downarrow}c^{\dagger}_{3,\uparrow}c^{\dagger}_{4,\uparrow}|\mathrm{vac}\rangle.

(S130)

III.1.1
No spin-down fermion

We first consider the simplest case of a single hole and
N-1
spin-up fermions, i.e., no spin-down fermions, corresponding to
S_{z}=\frac{N-1}{2}
.
The Hilbert space dimension of this sector is
N
.
We choose the following basis states

\displaystyle|j\rangle=(\prod_{j^{\prime}\neq j}c^{\dagger}_{j^{\prime},\uparrow})|\text{vac}\rangle=c^{\dagger}_{0,\uparrow}\cdots c^{\dagger}_{j-1,\uparrow}c^{\dagger}_{j+1,\uparrow}\cdots c^{\dagger}_{N-1,\uparrow}|\mathrm{vac}\rangle,

(S131)

where the hole is located at site
j
with
j=0,\ldots,N-1
.

The Hamiltonian (with hopping amplitude
t=1
) acts on these basis states as

\displaystyle\hat{H}|0\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\bigl(|1\rangle+(-1)^{N}|N-1\rangle\bigr),

\displaystyle\hat{H}|j\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\bigl(|j-1\rangle+|j+1\rangle\bigr),\quad j=1,\ldots,N-2,

\displaystyle\hat{H}|N-1\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\bigl((-1)^{N}|0\rangle+|N-2\rangle\bigr),

(S132)

where the phase factor
(-1)^{N}
arises from the fermionic sign accumulated when the hole hops across the boundary.

Since
\hat{H}
preserves translational symmetry, (unnormalized) eigenstates can be constructed as momentum states

\displaystyle|k\rangle=\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}e^{ikj}(T_{x})^{j}|0\rangle,

(S133)

where
T_{x}
denotes the translation operator that shifts all fermions to the right by one lattice site. The state
|k\rangle
is an eigenstate of the translation operator
T_{x}
with eigenvalue
e^{-ik}
. Under the action of the translation operator
T_{x}
, one has

\displaystyle T_{x}|0\rangle

\displaystyle=T_{x}\left(c^{\dagger}_{1,\uparrow}\cdots c^{\dagger}_{N-1,\uparrow}|\mathrm{vac}\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=c^{\dagger}_{2,\uparrow}\cdots c^{\dagger}_{N,\uparrow}|\mathrm{vac}\rangle

\displaystyle=(-1)^{N}c^{\dagger}_{0,\uparrow}c^{\dagger}_{2,\uparrow}\cdots c^{\dagger}_{N-1,\uparrow}|\mathrm{vac}\rangle

\displaystyle=(-1)^{N}|1\rangle,

(S134)

where the factor
(-1)^{N}
arises from reordering the fermionic creation operators based on the convention in Eq. (
S127
). The momentum eigenstate
|k\rangle
can thus be written as

\displaystyle|k\rangle=\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}e^{ikj}(-1)^{Nj}|j\rangle.

(S135)

It is straightforward to verify that
|k\rangle
is an eigenstate of
\hat{H}
provided
e^{ikN}=1
. Acting with
\hat{H}
yields

\displaystyle\hat{H}|k\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hat{H}|0\rangle+\sum_{j=1}^{N-2}e^{ikj}(-1)^{Nj}\hat{H}|j\rangle+e^{ik(N-1)}(-1)^{N(N-1)}\hat{H}|N-1\rangle

(S136)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\left(|1\rangle+(-1)^{N}|N-1\rangle\right)

\displaystyle-\sum_{j=1}^{N-2}e^{ikj}(-1)^{Nj}\left(|j-1\rangle+|j+1\rangle\right)

\displaystyle-e^{ik(N-1)}(-1)^{N(N-1)}\left((-1)^{N}|0\rangle+|N-2\rangle\right).

Rearranging the terms, one finds that all coefficients become proportional to
(e^{ik}+e^{-ik})
when
e^{ikN}=1
.
Consequently,

\displaystyle\hat{H}|k\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{N+1}\left(e^{ik}+e^{-ik}\right)|k\rangle

(S137)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2(-1)^{N+1}\cos(k)\,|k\rangle,

which gives the expected
2\cos(k)
dispersion of a hole in the ferromagnetic background (up to an overall sign determined by
N
).

Since the current operator
\hat{J}
commutes with
\hat{H}
as discussed in Sec.
II
, they share common eigenstates. It can be verified directly that
|k\rangle
is also an eigenstate of
\hat{J}
(see Eq. (
III.3.1
) for the proof), and

\displaystyle\frac{\bigl|\langle k^{\prime}|\hat{J}|k\rangle\bigr|^{2}}{\bigl|\langle k^{\prime}|k\rangle\bigr|^{2}}=\left(2\sin k\right)^{2}\delta_{k,k^{\prime}},

(S138)

where
\langle k^{\prime}|k\rangle=N\delta_{k,k^{\prime}}
.

Since the calculation of the Drude weight and the specific heat depends only on the energy spectrum, we label the eigenenergies by
m=0,\cdots,N-1
for simplicity.
For odd
N
,

k=\frac{2\pi m}{N}
.
For even
N
,
k=\frac{2\pi(m-N/2)}{N}=\frac{2\pi m}{N}-\pi
. Therefore, the energy spectrum can be expressed as
2\cos(\frac{2\pi m}{N})
with
m=0,\cdots,N-1
.

III.1.2
One spin-down fermion

Next, we consider the sector with one hole and one spin-down fermion, i.e.,
N-2
spin-up fermions, corresponding to
S_{z}=\frac{N-3}{2}
. The Hilbert space dimension of this sector is
N(N-1)
.

The
N(N-1)
Fock states can be constructed systematically as
|j,\alpha\rangle
(see Eq. (
S128
)). As an illustrative example, for
N=4
, the
12
Fock states are given below:

\displaystyle|j=0,\alpha=0\rangle

\displaystyle\equiv|0,-1,1,1\rangle,

\displaystyle|j=1,\alpha=0\rangle

\displaystyle\equiv|1,0,-1,1\rangle,

\displaystyle|j=2,\alpha=0\rangle

\displaystyle\equiv|1,1,0,-1\rangle,

\displaystyle|j=3,\alpha=0\rangle

\displaystyle\equiv|-1,1,1,0\rangle,

\displaystyle|j=0,\alpha=1\rangle

\displaystyle\equiv|0,1,-1,1\rangle,

\displaystyle|j=1,\alpha=1\rangle

\displaystyle\equiv|1,0,1,-1\rangle,

\displaystyle|j=2,\alpha=1\rangle

\displaystyle\equiv|-1,1,0,1\rangle,

\displaystyle|j=3,\alpha=1\rangle

\displaystyle\equiv|1,-1,1,0\rangle,

\displaystyle|j=0,\alpha=2\rangle

\displaystyle\equiv|0,1,1,-1\rangle,

\displaystyle|j=1,\alpha=2\rangle

\displaystyle\equiv|-1,0,1,1\rangle,

\displaystyle|j=2,\alpha=2\rangle

\displaystyle\equiv|1,-1,0,1\rangle,

\displaystyle|j=3,\alpha=2\rangle

\displaystyle\equiv|1,1,-1,0\rangle.

(S139)

Note that the Fock states
|j,\alpha\rangle
with the same
\alpha
share the same configuration on the
(N-1)
-site chain (i.e. the chain without the hole).

The Hamiltonian acts on the Fock states
|j,\alpha\rangle
as

\displaystyle\hat{H}|0,\alpha\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-|1,\alpha-1\rangle-(-1)^{N}|N-1,\alpha+1\rangle,

\displaystyle\hat{H}|j,\alpha\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-|j+1,\alpha-1\rangle-|j-1,\alpha+1\rangle,\qquad j=1,\ldots,N-2,

\displaystyle\hat{H}|N-1,\alpha\rangle

\displaystyle=
